## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 31 planned: backlash across the poses along a wall (does the held set hold, and does one calibration do)

- **Why.** Milestone 30 found backlash costs only on joints nothing holds, at one pose (hand reach 0.4 m, height 1.0 m), and treated every held joint's backlash as a fixed bias a tool-point calibration removes. A robot nailing a stud works from about 0.4 m to the top of its reach, and a little to either side of its stance. Two things can change with the pose: which joints are loose (the stack's random part), and the *sign* of each held joint's torque, which sets the side of the gap it rests on. Even with no sign change, the same joint-space bias lands at a different nose offset as the Jacobian changes, so a single calibration at one pose leaves a pose-dependent bias.
- **Grid, from an IK probe.** G1: heights 0.4–1.2 m in 0.2 m steps, hand targets shifted 0 and ±0.15 m along the wall (15 poses; at ±0.3 m and at 1.4 m the IK misses by 1.8–37 cm, so those are out of the stock G1's stance reach). H1: 0.6–1.6 m and the same shifts (the 0.4 m row reaches at dy 0 only). The probe ran the IK only; no torque, held set or stack number has been computed at any new pose.
- **Plan.** `src/buildbot_sheathing/pose_range.py`: (A) at each pose, milestone 30's static torques (gravity, the 3.4 kg nailer, a 25–50 N press at a 0.3 m nose, min-norm ground reaction) and held set (tau_f 0.2 N m, swept 0.05–0.5); (B) each pose's best single-2x stack at 12 bits, 5 arcmin, preloaded, with per-pose calibration (milestone 30's model at that pose's Jacobian); (C) the held joints' bias, each resting on the side its external load pushes it (half the gap, against the sign of the motor torque), carried to the nose and the depth point; with one tool-point calibration at milestone 30's pose, the residual bias at every other pose, split into the part from sign flips and the part from the Jacobian alone; (D) the stack at each pose with that residual bias as a fixed offset of every nail (a mean added to `ToolError`, zero by default so every earlier number reproduces); (E) a model-based calibration (the sign predicted from the torque model, the bias subtracted in joint space) as the remedy, and the largest backlash one calibration tolerates for a 2-point loss at the worst pose.
- **Hypotheses, registered before the run.**
  - **H115 (the held set is stable).** At every pose of the G1 grid, the preloaded 12-bit G1 with 5 arcmin and per-pose calibration stays within 3 points of that pose's no-backlash stack. Refuted if any pose loses more than 3.
  - **H116 (some held joint flips).** At least one G1 joint that moves the nose and is held at every grid pose changes the sign of its torque across the grid. Refuted if none does.
  - **H117 (one calibration is not enough).** With one tool-point calibration at milestone 30's pose and 5 arcmin, the residual held-joint bias moves the G1's nose along the wall by more than 0.3 mm at the worst grid pose. Refuted if 0.3 mm or less.

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 30 result: backlash costs only on joints nothing holds; for a G1 at the stud pose those are the two ankle rolls; H112 held, H113 and H114 refuted

`src/buildbot_sheathing/backlash.py` (new: a general tool offset, per-joint sigmas, MuJoCo static torques with a press at the nose, held/loose joints), `experiments/m30_backlash/` (run, results, figure), `tests/test_backlash.py` (5 tests), `reproduce.py` (m30 listed), `paper/draft.md` (section 5.11 paragraph, the tool-point limitation rewritten, a conclusion sentence), `paper/check_numbers.py` (12 new checks), README, PROPOSAL and project card.

- **Checks.** At zero lateral offset the general rows match milestone 29's to 4e-19 m and the stack reproduces 0.2637. Uniform per-joint backlash draws match the Gaussian tool model for one nail to 0.0015 (three windows, 200,000 draws).
- **Nose length, sourced.** The NR90AES1 is 12-11/16 in tall (Metabo HPT), so the grip sits 0.29–0.32 m behind the nose: the 11-bit G1 stack is 0.279–0.235 across that bracket, 12 bits 0.937–0.929. The 0.3 m of milestone 29 stands.
- **Handle offset (H114 refuted).** Only the vertical offset matters, through the hand's roll about the push axis: a grip 0.15 m *above* the drive axis costs the 11-bit G1 8.02 points, *below* it gains 4.08 (not registered); for the H1 the signs flip (+8.40 up, −11.37 down). Sideways offsets change nothing along the wall. At 12 bits the effects shrink to −1.56/+0.68 (G1).
- **Which joints are held.** At the stud pose with the 3.4 kg nailer and a 25–50 N press, 16–19 of the G1's 22 joints that move the nose keep one sign above 0.2 N m; the two ankle rolls carry no torque under the min-norm ground reaction and the wrist roll almost none. The H1 holds all 15 at 50 N.
- **The stack (12-bit G1, L 0.3 m).** Unpreloaded 5 arcmin: 0.9344 to 0.7646 (**H112 held**, −16.98 points); 10 arcmin 0.1759. Preloaded: 0.9134 at 5 arcmin, a 2.10-point loss at both press ranges (**H113 refuted**, bar 2), 0.8169 at 10. With every leg joint loose: 0.8870 at 5. With the ankle rolls biased to one side (a case added after the first run, not registered): 0.9344 at every gap to 25 arcmin.
- **The spec.** Largest backlash for 0.90 / 0.95 clean: unpreloaded 2.67 / – at 12 bits, 5.30 / 4.13 at 13; preloaded 6.24 / – and 12.28 / 9.64; ankle rolls biased, not binding below 60 arcmin at 13 bits. 12 bits alone never reach 0.95 (0.9344).
- **What it means.** Backlash is a gravity question: the arm joints are held by the nailer's weight and the press, and the error that reaches the nail comes from joints a standing robot leaves unloaded. A balance controller that keeps the ankle-roll torque one-signed (a small lateral shift of the centre of pressure) takes backlash out of the tool-point budget; one that does not makes a two-stage precision gearbox (3–7 arcmin) cost up to 13 points at 12 bits. The H1 has no ankle roll and holds everything.
- **Not measured.** No G1 backlash figure is published (swept 0–25 arcmin against catalogue classes); the ground-reaction split between the feet is the least-norm one, not a balance controller's (the legs-loose case bounds it); the holding threshold is swept 0.05–0.5 N m; the handle's offset from the drive axis is swept, not measured; with output-side encoders backlash would show as hunting rather than a static error (see `backlash-twin`).
- **Next.** The tool-point spec in the paper's abstract and highlights; link and mount deflection under the press (a deterministic part calibrates out, the press's scatter does not); the pose range along a wall, where held joints may change sign.

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 30 planned: backlash at the tool point, and which joints gravity and the press preload

- **Why.** Milestone 29 carried the G1's 11-bit quantisation to a 0.3 m nailer nose and found a stock G1 misses the single-2x stack (0.264), with 12-bit joints restoring 0.934. Quantisation is not the only joint error: planetary gear trains have backlash, and no G1 backlash figure is published (a search found teardown notes of two-stage planetaries at about 15:1 with output-side encoders, no number). Catalogue precision planetaries are rated 1, 3 or 5 arcmin (one stage) and 3, 5 or 7 arcmin (two stages); a search excerpt credits an unmodified quasi-direct-drive actuator with about 17 arcmin (not verified here: the thesis PDF host refused the proxy). Backlash only randomises a joint that is not held against one side of its gap: gravity on the arm and the 3.4 kg nailer, plus the press of the nose into the wall, load most joints one way at every nail, which turns their backlash into a fixed, calibratable bias. Milestone 29's nose was also a point on the hand's push axis; a real nailer's handle sits off the drive axis.
- **Nose geometry, sourced where possible.** Metabo HPT's product page gives the NR90AES1 (buildbot-fasten's nailer, 7.5 lb) as 20-3/4 x 4-15/16 x 12-11/16 in (L x W x H). The drive axis runs the tool's height, so a grip on the handle sits about 0.29–0.32 m behind the nose along the nail (milestone 29's 0.3 m is now inside a sourced bracket). The handle's offset from the drive axis is not on the sheet: swept 0–0.2 m, up and sideways.
- **Plan.** `src/buildbot_sheathing/backlash.py`: (A) per-joint tool-point rows for a general tool offset t (nose = hand + omega x t; depth point t + 44.4 mm along the nail), with per-joint sigmas, reducing to milestone 29's model at zero lateral offset; (B) the right arm's and whole body's static joint torques at milestone 29's pose (MuJoCo Menagerie G1 and H1, feet planted, gravity, the nailer's weight and a press force F on the right hand, ground reaction re-solved), F swept: a joint is *loose* if its torque changes sign as the press varies over [F/2, F] or stays below a holding threshold tau_f (swept 0.05–0.5 N m, not sourced); loose joints get a uniform error over the gap (sigma b/sqrt(12)), held joints a fixed bias (removed by calibration); (C) a Monte Carlo of uniform per-joint draws checked against the Gaussian tool model for one nail; (D) the best single-2x stack (milestone 29's, 0.7964 at the 0.9 mm point model) with each robot's error at 11–14 bits, backlash 0–25 arcmin, unpreloaded (every joint loose) and preloaded; (E) the tool-point spec: the largest backlash at each resolution that keeps the stack at 0.90 and 0.95.
- **Disclosed before registering.** Back-of-envelope only: 5 arcmin uniform is a 1-sigma of 0.024 deg against 12-bit quantisation's 0.025 deg; the hand's 0.1 deg roll at a 0.15 m offset is about 0.26 mm. No stack number with backlash or a lateral offset has been computed, and the torques have not been looked at.
- **Hypotheses, registered before the run.**
  - **H112 (unpreloaded backlash costs as much as a bit).** 5 arcmin of backlash on every G1 joint, none preloaded, costs the 12-bit G1 at L 0.3 m more than 5 points of the single-2x stack. Refuted if 5 or less.
  - **H113 (gravity and the press preload it away).** With the press swept 25–50 N and tau_f 0.2 N m, the same 5 arcmin costs the preloaded 12-bit G1 at most 2 points. Refuted if more than 2.
  - **H114 (the handle's offset is small).** A 0.15 m offset of the grip from the drive axis, in the worse of the up and sideways directions, moves the stock 11-bit G1's single-2x stack at L 0.3 m by less than 2 points. Refuted if 2 or more.

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 29 planned: the nailer's length and the nail's lean (what the 0.9 mm tool scatter leaves out)

- **Why.** Every clean-sheet figure since milestone 1 takes the tool error as `buildbot-fasten`'s 0.9 mm: the G1's 11-bit joint quantisation carried to the *hand point*, with the nailer's length ignored (an open gap in that project's audit), and the nail treated as a point on the sheathing surface. A nailer's nose sits about 0.3 m ahead of the grip (`buildbot-fasten`'s wall distance, not sourced), so the hand's rotation error moves the nose; and the nail must stay inside the stud for 10 diameters past the sheathing (milestone 2's band), 44 mm below the surface, so a leaning nail leaves the stud even when its head is in its window. Milestone 10 found the tool carries 45–47% of the single-2x misses, so the tool model matters more than any remedy.
- **Plan.** `src/buildbot_sheathing/tool_lean.py`: (A) the G1 and H1 hand's translation and rotation Jacobians (feet planted, MuJoCo Menagerie, `buildbot-fasten`'s pose), carried to the nose at a tool length L (0, 0.15, 0.3, 0.45 m) and to the shank 44.4 mm (7/16 in OSB plus 10 nail diameters) into the wall, giving a correlated pair (surface, depth) per nail and axis; (B) `tack.simulate` takes that pair: the panel-edge bound sees the surface point and the stud or plate bounds see the depth point (the old path unchanged when it is not given); a one-nail Monte Carlo is checked against the bivariate normal; (C) the best single-2x stack (measured studs, per-nail reads, mid push, milestone 27's 20 ms slide, milestone 28's 1/16 in shim stack: 0.7964) and the doubled-joint pipeline re-run with the robot's tool error at each L and joint error 1, 2 and 4 times the 11-bit quantisation; (D) an independent nail lean (the tool's own aim, a nail deflecting in the wood) swept 0–3° (1σ), and the lean the stack tolerates for a 2-point loss (bisection).
- **Disclosed before registering.** A probe of (A) at the G1's stud-height pose ran first: the hand rotates about 0.1° (1σ) from quantisation, and at L 0.3 m the nose's along-wall scatter is 1.31 mm against the hand's 0.93. No clean-sheet number had been computed.
- **Hypotheses, registered before the run.**
  - **H109 (the nailer's length matters).** With the G1's 11-bit error carried to a 0.3 m nose and the 44 mm depth, the best single-2x stack loses more than 2 points against the 0.9 mm hand-point model. Refuted if it loses 2 points or less.
  - **H110 (a 1° lean breaks the stack).** An independent nail lean of 1° (1σ, both axes) on top of the robot's error costs the best stack more than 5 points. Refuted if it costs 5 or less.
  - **H111 (doubled joints shrug it off).** The doubled-joint pipeline stays at or above 0.99 clean at a 2° lean. Refuted if below.

Breakaway is about 105 N, so at 63 N/m the set-point would sit 1.7 m behind the hand: the stock hand is a force source, and what stops the sheet is letting go.

- **The loop** (2,000 sheets, from either edge, stop within max(0.1 mm, half the last tap), breakaway ±10% a tap): 0.054–0.090 mm rms at 2 ms but 5–10 taps; 0.19–0.64 mm at 20 ms in 1.5–3 taps; 0.66–2.9 mm at 50 ms; 2.5–10 mm at 100 ms. **H104 held**: 3.855 mm at 100 ms, ratio 0.8.

The residual is put into the tolerance model as a Gaussian at its rms, though the loop's residual is closer to uniform.
- **Next.** The same treatment for the shim; a friction test or data set for OSB on SPF; a G1's measured slip-to-release latency.

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 27 planned: the slide as a mechanism (stick-slip on the ledger)

- **Why.** Milestones 10, 12 and 13 credit the slide (centre the sheet's gap along the ledger before tacking) with a 0.5 mm residual, and the paper's best single-2x stack (0.771 clean on measured studs) rests on it. Nobody has asked what leaves 0.5 mm. A seated sheet starts on static friction and slides on kinetic; a push-only hand overshoots by however far the sheet runs before the hand lets go.
- **Plan.** `src/buildbot_sheathing/slide_mech.py`: a one-axis stick-slip model of the sheet on the ledger and wall (milestone 3b's P = mu_l m g + mu_w F_n, split into static and kinetic), pushed by a hand of the stiffness the stock G1/H1 gains give along the wall (MuJoCo Menagerie models, milestone 3b's press pose), which ramps its force, detects the slip and releases after a latency. A closed form for a force-source hand checks it. Then a correct-from-either-side loop gives the slide residual, which goes back into milestone 12's grid. Friction: static 0.3–0.7 and mu_k/mu_s 0.5–0.9 swept (no OSB-on-lumber pair measured in a source this session could open).
- **Hypotheses, registered before the run.**
  - **H103 (the stock hand is a force source).** Along the wall the stock G1 and H1 hands are so soft that a single tap's travel is within 10% of a pure force source's at every reaction time swept. Refuted if any robot pose differs by more than 10%.
  - **H104 (reaction time sets the slide).** With mu_k/mu_s 0.8 and mu_s 0.4, the loop's rms residual exceeds 0.5 mm at a 100 ms reaction time. Refuted if it is 0.5 mm or less there.
  - **H105 (what the stack keeps).** At a 500 Hz loop with 20 ms latency, milestone 12's best single-2x stack (0.771) stays within 2 points with the slide residual from the mechanism in place of 0.5 mm, at mu_k/mu_s 0.8. Refuted if it loses more than 2 points.

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 21 result: nails that lose strength with dissipated energy lower every wall alike, and lift the decayed shares; the 3 in wall stays above 70% at code strength

`experiments/m21_degrading/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, check_diag.py, check_diag.txt, figure.py, figure.png); `wall_cyclic.Saws(gamma=...)` and `Saws.degrading`; `tests/test_wall_degrading.py` (5 tests). About 35 min on four CPUs (72 CUREE runs of 3,000–9,300 steps, 8 monotonic pushes).

- **H98 refuted as registered, by a mechanism, not a bug.** γ = ∞ reproduces milestone 20 exactly (0 difference on the single-spring CUREE trace, the OpenSees trace and the registered-point wall peaks). But a monotonic push at γ 5 moves the wall peaks by up to **1.6e-5 FU**, not ≤ 1e-9: in a wall, 203–381 of the 218–454 springs reverse at least once after yielding during a one-way push (the cross spring of each oriented pair, and springs that unload after a neighbour snaps), so the rule does start counting their work. It does so after the peak (`check_diag.txt`: s falls to 0.69–0.83 by 150 mm), so the monotonic references stand.
- **H99 refuted as scripted, by the check.** The hand recomputation in `run.py` left out the rule's "s never rises" clamp, so it disagreed by 9e-3 FU while a spring unloaded. With the clamp (`check_diag.py`) the force on the envelope equals s × E(d) to **4e-16 FU** and s matches the hand value exactly.
- **H100 held.** At code-strength joints (m 1, λ 1, 1/16 in gap) the decayed 3-12 wall keeps **70.6, 70.9, 71.1, 72.4 and 73.2%** at γ ∞, 100, 50, 20 and 10 (73.5% at 5).
- **H101 held, in the direction nobody wanted.** Degradation moves each decayed share by **+0.3 to +2.9 points**: up, not down. At m 0.25 the 6-12 share rises 10 points (57.9% to 68.1%) by γ 5.
- **H102 held.** No point with m ≥ 0.75 within 10 points of both shares at any γ.
- **Why the shares rise.** Damage follows the energy each nail dissipates, and an intact wall dissipates more in its sheathing nails than a decayed one whose capacity is set by the frame sliding on its end nails. At m 0.25, γ 5, the intact walls' sheathing nails keep 71–77% of their strength and the decayed walls' 86–96%. At m 1 all four walls lose about alike (s 0.69–0.74), and the shares still rise 1.5–2.9 points.
- **The cost to the intact walls.** Their CUREE peaks fall to about **97, 94, 85, 75 and 63%** of their monotonic peaks at γ 100, 50, 20, 10 and 5 (within a point at every m), and the intact ratio 3-12/6-12 stays at 1.90–1.94 (tested 1.87). Rates that would matter at all therefore also predict cyclic peaks well below monotonic for the intact walls.
- **Best fits get worse with damage.** The best point at every γ is m 0.25; its rms grows from **2.1 points** (no degradation) to 2.5, 3.1, 4.3, 6.4 and 8.7.
- **What it means for the programme.** Damage spread by dissipated energy, the standard cumulative-damage term in hysteretic wall models, cannot produce the 34%: it scales intact and decayed walls together. What is left must single out the decayed wall: tear-out of the few nails left along its unnailed bottom edge, or the middle stud splitting (both reported by Weiss), or end nails that are genuinely weak. The first two need a local failure model (a nail that fails by edge distance and cycle count, from tested curves of nails at an unsupported panel edge); the third a tested 16d end-nail ultimate. For the robot nothing moves.
- **Inputs not sourced:** γ (swept; no calibrated value for nails in OSB was found, and the Stanford repository holding the CUREE reports did not resolve from this session); the end-nail joint's curve (as milestones 19–20). One rule for every spring.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 21 planned: Weiss's wall with nails that lose strength as they dissipate energy

Written before any milestone-21 wall load was computed (only the single-nail calibration below was run). Inputs: milestone 20's wall, nails, rods, CUREE history and Δ (`experiments/m20_cyclic/results.json`).

- **Why.** Milestone 20 found that SAWS pinching alone leaves the decayed 3 in wall at 70.6% kept under the CUREE history (tested 34%). SAWS has no term for damage that grows with cycles (nail fatigue, tear-out), which Anderson et al. (2007) and Gatto and Uang (2003) saw. This step adds the standard energy-based strength degradation and asks how much of it the 34% would need, and whether that much is compatible with the intact walls.
- **Model.** `wall_cyclic.Saws(gamma=...)`: every spring's envelope and pinching lines are scaled by s = 1 − E/E_t, E the work done on the spring from its first reversal after yielding (a monotonic push is never degraded), E_t = γ FU Δu of that spring. This is the c = 1 continuous form of Rahnama and Krawinkler (1993)'s rule as used in the Ibarra, Medina and Krawinkler (2005) model. Unloading and reloading stiffness are not degraded. γ is **not sourced** (no calibrated value for nails in OSB was found this session): it is swept over ∞, 100, 50, 20, 10, 5. Calibration on one Anderson nail through the CUREE history at Δ = 2Δu (work 13.8 FU Δu in all): its envelope peak falls to 0.96, 0.91, 0.81, 0.73 and 0.61 of the undegraded peak at γ 100, 50, 20, 10, 5.
- **H98 (code check: reduces to milestone 20).** γ = ∞ reproduces milestone 20's `Saws` exactly (0 difference) on the single-spring CUREE trace and milestone 20's registered-point wall peaks to 1e-9; at any γ a monotonic push is unchanged.
- **H99 (code check: the rule).** On a single spring, whenever it is on its envelope, its force equals s × the undegraded envelope with s recomputed from its own force–displacement history, to 1e-9 FU.
- **H100 (moderate damage does not take the 3 in wall to 34%).** At the registered point (m 1, λ 1, gap 1/16 in, cap 2.55 FU), the decayed 3-12 wall keeps at least 50% for every γ ≥ 10.
- **H101 (damage scales intact and decayed walls alike).** At the registered point, for every γ in the grid, each decayed share moves by at most 10 points from its γ = ∞ value.
- **H102 (no fit near code strength).** Over m ∈ {0.25, 0.47, 1.0}, λ 1, gap 1/16 in, and the γ grid, no point with m ≥ 0.75 puts both decayed shares within 10 points of the tested 56.5% and 33.9%.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the intact walls' cyclic peaks against their monotonic peaks at each γ (an intact wall far below its monotonic peak is a sign γ is too low for Weiss's materials), the intact ratio 3-12/6-12 (tested 1.87), the best fit at each γ.
- **Limits known in advance.** γ is swept, not sourced; one rule for every spring (sheathing nails and end-nail joints alike); damage is spread over the spring, not tied to a failure mode (tear-out at the unsupported bottom edge would hit the bottom nails first); Anderson's nail envelope was itself fitted to cyclic tests, so part of a nail's cyclic damage is already in it and any γ here is extra damage.

- **H96 held.** At code-strength joints, the rods at their design value and a 1/16 in gap, the decayed 3-12 wall keeps **70.6%** cyclic against 70.5% monotonic (+0.1 point); the decayed 6-12 wall 77.8% against 78.3%. Tested: 34% and 57%.
- **H97 held.** No grid point with m ≥ 0.75 is within 10 points of both tested shares under cyclic loading; the closest (m 0.75, λ 0.25, 1/4 in gap) keeps 76.3% and 43.3% (rms 15.5 points).
- **Across the grid** cyclic loading moves a decayed share by a median **0.2 point** and at most 3.0 points (m 0.25, λ 0.25, 1/4 in gap). The best cyclic fit is m 0.25, λ 1, no gap: 57.6% and 31.4% (rms 1.9 points), decay ratio 1.04 (tested 1.12), the end nails at a quarter of NDS again. Post hoc, with this envelope one point at m 0.47 also fits (λ 0.25, 1/4 in gap: 55.5% and 30.0%, rms 2.9), where milestone 19's nail gave 63.4% and 38.3%; that comes from the nail's envelope, not the cycles (the monotonic push there gives 54.8% and 30.8%).
- **Sensitivity (not scored).** Anderson's other three rows move the registered point's shares by under a point; Folz and Filiatrault's set (milder pinching, r3 1.4) keeps 74.3% and 66.2% cyclic against 75.4% and 66.9% monotonic; Δ from the radial model's push and 1 mm steps change nothing to 0.1 point. Largest residual over every run 1e-7 FU.
- **Where the cycles do act: the rods.** In the decayed 3-12 wall the anchor rods reach their cap and the holes crush, to **25–28 mm** on one side by the end of the history (intact 3-12: 0.4 mm; 6-12: none). The extra slack comes after the peak, so it does not lower it.
- **What it means for the programme.** With the hysteresis model Weiss's own MCASHEW2 belongs to, cyclic loading does not take the 3 in decayed wall from 70% to 34%: its envelope peak is the monotonic peak. Two explanations are left. The end nails are weak (a quarter of NDS fits every model since milestone 18), or the walls lose capacity through damage this class of model has no term for: nail fatigue and tear-out along the unsupported bottom edge, which Anderson et al. saw grow with cycles, and the middle stud splitting. A tested 16d end-nail ultimate decides the first; a degrading envelope (cumulative-damage SAWS variants, or a tested decayed-wall nail curve) tests the second. For the robot nothing moves.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the end-nail joint's curve (shape borrowed from the sheathing nail, strength m and slip scale λ swept); Anderson's nail is 0.113 in against Weiss's 0.131 in 8d common (only shares are compared); Δ from this model's own push.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 20 planned: Weiss's wall under the CUREE protocol, with pinching nails

Written before any milestone-20 load was computed. Inputs: milestone 19's wall (`wall_push.PushWall` bodies: 8 by 8 ft, two sheets, seven studs, hold-downs on the double end studs, the anchor rods), milestone 17's NDS end-nail ratio (1.297 F) and milestone 18's rod path (2.55 F).

- **Why.** Milestone 19 left two explanations for Weiss's 3 in decayed wall (34% kept): end nails at about a quarter of their NDS ratio, or what a monotonic push leaves out. Weiss tested cyclically (ASTM E2126, CUREE). This step loads the same wall with the CUREE basic history and hysteretic fasteners, so the second explanation is tested directly.
- **New sourced inputs.** (1) The CUREE basic loading history, exactly as Krawinkler et al. (2001, CUREE W-02, Sec. 1.1) define it: six initiation cycles at 0.05Δ; primaries at 0.075, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4, 0.7 and 1.0Δ followed by 6, 6, 3, 3, 2, 2 and 2 trailing cycles at 75% of the primary; then primaries rising by 0.5Δ, each with two trailing cycles; Δ = 0.6 Δm, with Δm the monotonic drift where the load first falls below 0.8 of its peak. (2) The ten-parameter SAWS hysteresis (Folz and Filiatrault 2001: Foschi envelope, unloading at r3 K0, pinching lines through ±FI at r4 K0, reloading at K0 (F0/(K0 δmax))^α towards β times the last excursion) with the parameters Anderson et al. (2007, Wood and Fiber Science 39(4), Table 4) fitted to cyclic single-nail tests of 0.113 in nails through 7/16 in OSB into Douglas fir-larch, Weiss's sheathing and framing species: at fyb 115 ksi K0 2,851 lbf/in, r1 0.037, r2 −0.069, r3 3.91, r4 0.028, F0 249 lbf, FI 32.4 lbf, Δu 0.425 in, α 0.265, β 1.15. Folz and Filiatrault's own set (8d in 9.5 mm OSB, as tabulated by Ghazinader 2024, Table 2) is the alternate.
- **Model** (`src/buildbot_sheathing/wall_cyclic.py`). Milestone 19's bodies and penalties. Each sheathing nail is an oriented spring pair (Judd and Fonseca 2005): two uncoupled SAWS springs along and across the direction the nail first slips. Each stud-to-plate joint laterally is a SAWS spring with forces scaled by m × 1.297 per end nail and displacements by λ (shape borrowed from the sheathing nail, as milestone 19). Each anchor rod bears after a gap g in a hole that crushes: past its cap c the hole lengthens, so later cycles have more slack. Displacement control of the top plate (no rotation), Newton on the out-of-balance force at each 0.5 mm step from the committed fastener states. Peak = the mean of the largest positive and negative loads over the history (the envelope's peaks). Loads in units of FU, the nail's envelope peak.
- **H93 (code check: the hysteresis).** On a single spring driven through the CUREE history (Δ = 2 Δu), `wall_cyclic`'s SAWS matches OpenSees' `SAWSMaterial` (the port of Folz's code) to an rms difference of at most 2% of FU over the whole history, for both parameter sets. Refuted otherwise (a bug, or a documented difference in a rule).
- **H94 (code check: the pair against the radial spring).** Pushed monotonically, the hysteretic wall's peak is within 3% of milestone 19's path-independent `PushWall` with the same Foschi envelope as a radial spring, for the four walls at m 1, λ 1, cap 2.55 F, no gap.
- **H95 (cyclic loading barely moves the intact walls).** Under the CUREE history the intact 6-12 and 3-12 walls' peaks are within 10% of their monotonic peaks at m 1, λ 1, cap 2.55 F, gap 1/16 in.
- **H96 (cyclic loading does not explain the 3 in wall).** At the same point, the decayed 3-12 wall's share kept under the CUREE history is within 10 points of its monotonic share (so cyclic loading does not bring 70% down to 34%).
- **H97 (no fit near code strength, cyclic).** Over m ∈ {0.25, 0.47, 0.75, 1.0}, λ ∈ {0.25, 1}, g ∈ {0, 1/16, 1/4} in (cap 2.55 F), no point with m ≥ 0.75 puts both cyclic decayed shares within 10 points of the tested 56.5% and 33.9%. Expected held; refuted would mean cyclic loading reopens code-strength joints as an explanation.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the cyclic and monotonic shares and decay ratios over the grid; the best cyclic fit; the step size (0.5 against 1 mm) at one point; the rod hole's growth; Δ for each schedule; Anderson's other fyb rows and Folz and Filiatrault's set at the registered point.
- **Limits known in advance.** The SAWS envelope does not degrade with cycles beyond what β and the pinching give (no low-cycle fatigue or tear-out, which Weiss and Anderson saw); Anderson's nail is 0.113 in, Weiss's 8d common 0.131 in, so only shares are compared; rigid studs and sheets; the end-nail joint's shape is borrowed; Δ comes from this model's own monotonic push, not from Weiss's protocol records.

At that point 79% of the decayed 6-12 wall's stored energy at peak is in sheathing nails and 21% in end-nail joints; 56% of the sheathing nails' share is on the double end studs (Weiss's failure line), 26% on the middle stud.
- **Numerics.** Every peak settled to 1e-7 Fp; after the peak some walls snap and Newton cannot settle every step (largest residual 3.6e2 Fp). Median share of nails unloading before the peak 0%, 90th percentile 5.2%, largest 100% (a few runs peak after a snap; the path-independent reading counts them on their backbone).
- **What it means for the programme.** Slack at the rods and the order in which fasteners peak are ruled out under monotonic loading. Two explanations are left: the 16d end-nailed joints really carry about a quarter of their NDS ratio at ultimate in these walls, or the 34% comes from what a monotonic model leaves out (cyclic tear-out at the unsupported bottom edge, the middle stud splitting). A tested end-nail ultimate decides the first; a cyclic (CUREE) push with pinching, which `wall_push` can now host, tests the second. For the robot nothing moves: milestone 17 and 18's escaped-miss figures stand.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the end-nail joint's backbone (strength m and slip scale λ swept, shape borrowed from the 8d nail); δp (swept 6–12 mm, as milestone 16); the rod's stiffness is the NDS design modulus.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 19 planned: Weiss's wall pushed over with softening nails and slack at the anchor rods

Written before any milestone-19 load was computed. Inputs: milestone 17's wall (`wall_lp.Wall` geometry: 8 by 8 ft, two sheets, seven studs, hold-downs on the double end studs), milestone 16's sourced nail backbone (8d through 7/16 in OSB, Shameli Derakshan et al. 2019: Fp 0.972 kN, K at 0.4 Fp 0.496 kN/mm, Δu 28.7 mm; δp not tabulated, swept), milestone 17's NDS end-nail ratio (1.297 F per 16d end nail) and milestone 18's rod path (design value 2.55 F).

- **Why.** Milestones 17 and 18 are rigid-plastic: every fastener reaches its capacity at once and nothing has slack. Milestone 18 left two things that model cannot show: slack in the anchor rod's hole, which lets the end studs slide before the rod bears, and joints that peak at a different slip from the sheathing nails, so one set is past its peak before the other arrives. This step builds the same wall as a displacement model and pushes it over.
- **Model** (`src/buildbot_sheathing/wall_push.py`). The bodies of `wall_lp.Wall` (top plate, studs, sheets; small rigid motions). Sheathing nails: milestone 16's backbone along each nail's slip. Stud-to-plate joints: a 1-D backbone along the wall with peak m × 1.297 Fp per end nail and slip scale λ (δp_joint = λ δp, Δu_joint = λ Δu, same shape); vertically, stiff one-sided bearing, free opening; at the held-down end-stud bottoms a stiff two-sided tie. Anchor rod: 1-D along the wall at each held-down bottom, zero force over a gap g, then the NDS load/slip modulus for a dowel in wood with a steel side member (2 × 270,000 D^1.5 lb/in, D 5/8 in: about 47 kN/mm) up to a cap c Fp, elastic-plastic. Top plate pushed along the wall without rotation to 150 mm in 0.5 mm steps; at each step the bodies sit at the least-energy state (damped Newton from the last step), as milestone 16. Loads in units of Fp.
- **H87 (code check: the rigid-plastic limit).** With every spring elastic-perfectly-plastic at a 0.1 mm yield slip, no gap, and stiff penalties, the pushover's load at 150 mm is within 2% of `Wall.capacity` (same joint strength and hd_shear) for the intact and decayed 6-12 and 3-12 walls at m 0.47 and 1.0, rod cap 0 and 2.55. Refuted otherwise (a bug).
- **H88 (code check: milestone 16).** One sheet on milestone 15's four studs, joints pinned (stiff penalties), hold-downs at both end studs, milestone 16's base backbone (δp 12 mm): the peak is within 1% of `softnail.pushover` for the intact sheet and the Weiss pattern.
- **H89 (softening alone changes little).** At δp 12 mm, λ = 1, m = 1 (NDS), rod cap 2.55 F and no gap, both decayed shares are within 5 points of the rigid-plastic LP's at the same joint strength and rod path (milestone 16's finding for one sheet, carried to the wall).
- **H90 (slack in a standard hole is not enough).** At m = 0.47, λ = 1, cap 2.55 F, opening the gap from 0 to 1/16 in (a standard oversize bolt hole) lowers each decayed share by at most 5 points.
- **H91 (stiff joints that peak early).** At m = 1, cap 2.55 F, no gap, joints that peak at a quarter of the sheathing nails' slip (λ = 0.25) bring the decayed 3-12 wall below 50% kept.
- **H92 (a fit near code strength).** Over m ∈ {0.25, 0.47, 0.75, 1.0}, λ ∈ {0.25, 0.5, 1}, g ∈ {0, 1/32, 1/16, 1/8, 1/4} in, cap ∈ {2.55, 5.1} F and δp ∈ {6, 12} mm, some point with m ≥ 0.75 puts both decayed shares within 10 points of the tested 56.5% and 33.9%. Expected refuted.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the full grid's shares and decay ratio, the best-fitting point, drift at peak against Weiss's reported drifts where the thesis tabulates them, and at the best point the share of joint and nail work by member.
- **Limits known in advance.** Monotonic push against cyclic tests; nail and joint unloading read back down the backbone (path-independent, counted as in milestone 16); rigid studs and sheets; the end-nail joint backbone's shape is borrowed from the sheathing nail (no tested 16d end-nail curve found yet), so λ and m are swept, not sourced; the rod's stiffness is a design modulus.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 18 result: the hold-downs' anchor rods stop Weiss's decayed wall sliding, and then no joint strength fits her 3 in wall

`experiments/m18_holddown_shear/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `wall_lp.Wall.capacity(hd_shear=...)`; 3 new tests in `tests/test_wall_lp.py`. About 4.5 min on four CPUs.

- **Input this step added:** the shear path through each HTT4's 5/8 in anchor rod. Its NDS yield-limit design value, bearing in the 1.5 in DF-L sill with a steel side member: **617 lb** (1/8 in steel), 729 lb (1/4 in), 1,137 lb (1/2 in), all mode II. At the 242 lb a sheathing nail that the controls imply, 617 lb is **2.55 F**.
- **H81 held.** s = 0 gives milestone 17's four registered capacities exactly; capacity never falls as s rises; s = inf matches s = 10,000 F to 7e-16.
- **H82 refuted, as expected.** A rigid shear path at NDS joints: the decayed walls keep 79.5% and **73.5%** (milestone 17: 79.5%, 63.4%; tested 57%, 34%).
- **H83 and H84 refuted.** With a rigid path, no end-nail strength from 0.05 to 1.50 of the NDS ratio fits. The best, 0.17, keeps 68.9% and 63.6% (rms **22.8 points**), and the decayed 3-12 wall comes out at 1.77 times the decayed 6-12 (tested 1.12). With the end studs held, the decayed 3-12 wall has to shear its dense nails along the end studs, so the schedule matters again, far more than in the tests.
- **H85 held, at a fit that misses.** At that fit, 69% of the decayed 6-12 wall's sheathing-nail work is on the double end studs (85% for 3-12), which is the failure line Weiss reported. Milestone 17's sliding fit put none there.
- **H86 held.** At milestone 17's fit (0.47 of the NDS ratio) the end studs stop sliding once each rod carries **1.75 F (about 420 lb)**. That is below the rod path's own design value, and a tested ultimate would be higher still.
- **Post hoc (not registered): a trade-off.** The decayed walls' capacity is close to the frame's sliding resistance: 18 end nails plus two rods. Fits within 5 points rms exist for s up to **4 F (about 970 lb a rod)**, and the end-nail multiple they need falls as s rises: 0.45 at s = 0, 0.25 at the rod's design value (2.55 F), 0.05 at s = 4 F. At 0.25, each 16d end nail carries about **78 lb** at the tests' scale, below its own NDS design value (94 lb with C_eg). So milestone 17's explanation, that the decayed wall slides on its sill nails, needs the anchor rods to carry less than their design value and the end nails less than theirs. Code-level fastener strengths do not support both.
- **Escaped misses unchanged.** On the first 2,000 walls: mean loss 2.74% and 99th percentile 10.77% in milestone 17's model, and **2.13%** and **9.02%** with a rigid path at the 0.17 fit. Under every anchorage model tried, an escaped miss costs 2–3% of a wall on average and about 9–11% at the 99th percentile.
- **What it means for the programme.** A rigid-plastic, small-displacement model of Weiss's wall built from sourced strengths does not reproduce her 3 in decayed wall (34% kept). Once the rods' shear is in, it keeps 64–74%. The candidates left are ones this model cannot represent: slack in the anchor rod's hole, which lets the wall slide before the rod bears and is worth more under cyclic loading; the middle stud splitting, which Weiss reports; and sheathing nails tearing out of the unsupported bottom edge under reversed loads. Next: a displacement-based (pushover) wall with a slip gap at the rods, using milestone 16's nail springs. A tested ultimate for 16d end-nailed joints is still wanted.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the steel side member's thickness (swept 1/8–1/2 in); the rod path is a design equation, not a tested ultimate (only its design value is used, and a tested ultimate would only strengthen H86); the rigid-plastic path engages with no slack.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 18 planned: does the hold-down's shear path stop Weiss's decayed wall sliding, and what joint strength fits then?

Written before any milestone-18 capacity was computed. Inputs: milestone 17's wall (`wall_lp.Wall`, Weiss's 8 by 8 ft wall), its NDS joint ratio (1.297 F per 16d end nail) and its post hoc fit (0.47 times that ratio).

- **Why.** Milestone 17's fit has the decayed wall slide on its sill: every stud's end nails shear and nothing else moves. But in Weiss's set-up (Ch. 4.3.3–4.3.4) each double end stud is nailed to an HTT4 hold-down whose 5/8 in anchor rod passes through the sill into the steel test beam, so the end studs cannot slide along the sill without bending that rod and crushing the sill around it. Milestone 17 modelled the hold-downs as vertical ties only and left this "real load path" out, and its fit matched Weiss's spacing finding but not her reported failure (sheathing nails pulling out along the double end studs). This step adds it.
- **Model change.** `Wall.capacity(..., hd_shear=s)`: at each held-down stud bottom the lateral joint carries s F on top of its end nails (rigid-plastic; `s=inf`: no slide at all). `s=0` is milestone 17's model.
- **Sourced scale for s.** The NDS yield-limit (dowel) equations for a 5/8 in rod (F_yb 45,000 psi) bearing parallel to grain in a 1.5 in DF-L sill (G 0.50) with a steel side member (the beam flange or the HTT4 seat), single shear, R_d for D > 1/4 in. A design value, so the tested ultimate is larger by an unknown factor: reported as 1 to 5 times Z, swept rather than picked, and converted to F with milestone 17's implied 238–246 lb per sheathing nail.
- **H81 (code check).** `hd_shear=0` reproduces milestone 17's registered capacities (intact and decayed, both schedules, NDS joints) to 1e-9 relative; capacity never falls as s rises; `s=inf` gives the same capacity as s set above the frame's total nail capacity. Refuted otherwise (a bug).
- **H82 (the shear path alone).** At NDS-ratio joints with a rigid shear path, the decayed walls keep 47–67% (6-12) and 24–44% (3-12). Expected refuted: adding a load path cannot lower a decayed capacity, and milestone 17 already kept 79.5% and 63.4%.
- **H83 (one joint strength still fits).** With a rigid shear path, some single multiple of the NDS joint ratio in 0.05–1.50 fits both tested shares (56.5%, 33.9%) to an rms of at most 5 points.
- **H84 (the schedule matters again).** At H83's best fit, decay 3-12 over decay 6-12 lies in 1.02–1.25 (tested 1.12; milestone 17's fit gave 1.00).
- **H85 (where it fails).** At H83's best fit, the decayed 6-12 wall's mechanism spends at least half its sheathing-nail dissipation in nails on the two double end studs (Weiss's reported failure line).
- **H86 (the real rod locks the slide).** The shear capacity at which, at 0.47 times the NDS ratio, the decayed 6-12 wall stops sliding as a whole (the end studs' slide falls below half the top plate's) is at most the NDS design value Z of the anchor-rod path, in F.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the share kept and decay ratio over s (0, 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 F and rigid) times the joint multiple (0.05–1.50); the mechanism at each fit; the tested per-wall spread (Table 5-1) beside the fit; escaped misses (milestone 17's H80) rerun at the new fit on a 2,000-wall subsample.
- **Limits known in advance.** As milestone 17: rigid-plastic, monotonic, rigid studs, cyclic tests of two or three walls a group. The rod's shear path is a dowel design equation, not a tested ultimate; the steel side member's thickness is not given in the thesis text we read, so the steel's bearing is taken as not governing (checked by sweeping its thickness).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 17 result: Weiss's own wall at code-strength joints still keeps 80%; with end nails at about half the NDS ratio the decayed wall slides on its sill and matches both tested groups

`experiments/m17_weiss_wall/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `src/buildbot_sheathing/wall_lp.py` (milestone 15's limit analysis for a wall of several sheets on shared studs); `tests/test_wall_lp.py` (5 tests). About 5 min on four CPUs (about 9,300 LPs).

- **Input this step added:** Weiss's thesis full text (open access at WSU), so the wall is built as tested: 8 by 8 ft, two sheets on a single middle stud, hold-downs only on the double end studs, every nail below 12 in omitted; Table 5-2's means (control 6-12 4,688 lb, decay 2,649 lb, 57% kept; control 3-12 8,775 lb, decay 2,974 lb, 34% kept).
- **H75 held.** One sheet in the wall LP matches milestone 15's `Frame` to 0.0 in 8 cases. One deviation, noted in results.txt: `Frame` has no rotation-free top plate, so the check used its free and slide-only modes.
- **H76 held.** Intact 3-12 over 6-12: 1.931 against the tested 1.872 (+3.2%). Absolute, at the tested 8d-in-OSB peak (0.972 kN, SPF framing): 4,168 lb against 4,688 lb (0.89) and 8,049 against 8,775 lb (0.92). The tests imply 238–246 lb a sheathing nail in DF-L.
- **H77, H78, H79 refuted.** At NDS-ratio joints (1.297 sheathing nails per 16d end nail, 7/16 in OSB) the decayed 6-12 wall keeps 79.5% and the 3-12 wall 63.4% (tested 57% and 34%), and decay 3-12 over 6-12 is 1.54 (tested 1.12). So Weiss's anchorage, with the shared stud held down by two end nails alone, does not explain her walls at code-strength joints: the lone sheet with its own hold-downs kept 78.9%. No hold-down variant gets there either: with none the intact walls fall to 13.6 and 28.3 F (71% and 77% of the registered values) and the decayed wall keeps 50–51%. Finite hold-downs, withdrawal, sheet-to-sheet bearing, the top-plate boundary and the inclusive or perimeter-only reading of the decay pattern all keep 60–80%.
- **H80 held.** With every miss left in (three reads, measured studs, two sheets a wall, 10,000 walls) the wall's mean loss is 2.73% and its 99th-percentile loss 10.41%, against milestone 14's 2.34% and 11.49% a sheet. At the fitted joints (below): 2.67% and 10.19%. The escaped-miss figures the programme reports do not depend on the anchorage or the joint strength.
- **Post hoc (section F, not registered): one joint strength fits both tested groups.** Below 0.6 of the NDS ratio, the LP's least-work mechanism for the decayed wall is the whole wall sliding on its sill: every stud's end nails shear and nothing else moves (section E), so the capacity is 18 end nails' worth whatever the sheathing schedule. That reproduces Weiss's finding that nail spacing "does not have a significant influence" once the bottom nails are gone. A least-squares fit to the two tested shares gives **0.47 times the NDS ratio**: 58.2% and 30.2% kept (tested 56.5% and 33.9%; rms 2.9 points), with a decay ratio of 1.00 against the tested 1.12. The intact walls barely notice (18.9 against 19.07 F). Read the other way, the decayed walls carried 147–165 lb per 16d end nail, about the nail's NDS design value without the end-grain factor, while the sheathing nails reached about 3.3 times theirs. That is one fitted parameter against two tested numbers plus the spacing trend, not a validation. It also leaves out a real load path, shear through the hold-down and the anchor bolts, so the joints may be weaker still. And it fits Weiss's report that the middle stud slid off its sill nails, not her report that the failure ran along the double studs.
- **What it means for the programme.** The candidate milestone 15 left as "joints or fasteners far weaker at ultimate than the NDS ratio implies" is now the one that fits a tested wall, and for a specific joint: the stud-to-sill end nails. For the robot, the escaped-miss losses stand under every joint model tried (H80). The weak link that decides how much a wall with a dead bottom foot keeps is a framing joint the framer nails, not the sheathing schedule. Next: a tested ultimate for 16d end-nailed stud-to-plate joints, to check the 0.47 independently, and the hold-down's and anchor bolts' shear path.
- **Inputs not sourced:** joint ratios are NDS design (yield-limit) values; the 0.47 is fitted. Rigid-plastic, monotonic, rigid studs (Weiss's middle studs split), against cyclic tests of two or three walls a group.

The decayed walls failed by lateral pull-out of the sheathing nails along the double end studs, the panels "acted together as one", and the middle stud split or slid off its two end nails (Ch. 5.2.3). Nail schedule barely mattered once the bottom nails were gone (3-12 decay only 1.12 times 6-12 decay, against 1.87 for the controls).

HTT4 hold-downs: rigid vertical ties at the double end studs' bottoms. Top plate: translation only along the wall and up, no rotation (MCASHEW2's boundary condition in Weiss Ch. 3.2). ## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 16 planned: do softening nails explain why the model understated Weiss's walls?

Written before any milestone-16 capacity was computed. Inputs: milestone 14's nail layout, kinematics (pinned framing, rigid sheet, full anchorage) and miss patterns (20,000 sheets, seed 7, measured studs).

- **Why.** Milestones 14 and 15 scored missing nails with rigid-plastic fasteners: every nail reaches the same capacity F at any slip, so the sheet's capacity is the sum over nails of F times its share of the slip. Real nails peak at a slip of some millimetres and then soften, so at the sheet's peak the corner nails, which slip most, are already past their peak while the nails near the sheet's centre of rotation have not reached theirs. Removing the bottom foot's nails moves the sheet's centre of rotation up and makes the remaining nails' slips more unequal, which a rigid-plastic model cannot see. Milestone 15 ruled out the stud-to-plate joints at code strength; this is the second of the three candidates it left (anchorage, weaker joints or fasteners, softening nails).
- **Model.** Milestone 14's kinematics with each nail a nonlinear spring along its slip (the oriented-spring idealisation behind CASHEW, Folz and Filiatrault 2001): load P(δ) = (F0 + r1 K0 δ)(1 − exp(−K0 δ / F0)) up to the peak slip δp, then a straight fall through 0.8 Fp at Δu to zero. Displacement-controlled pushover of the top plate to 150 mm; at each step the sheet's (u, v, θ) minimises the springs' energy (Newton from the last step, monotonic loading taken as path-independent; the fraction of nails that unload is reported). Capacity is the peak racking load. Backbone from tests of 8d common nails through 7/16 in OSB into SPF (Shameli Derakshan, Zhou and Ni 2019, Table 2; six replicates a group): Fp 0.90–1.06 kN monotonic (1.01–1.26 cyclic), secant stiffness at 0.4 Fp 0.40–0.57 kN/mm (cyclic; the monotonic K is unreliable, the authors say), Δu 26.7–31.2 mm monotonic and 18.9–21.4 mm cyclic. The slip at peak δp is not tabulated: swept 6–15 mm (r1 set so the curve peaks there). Capacity *ratios* do not depend on Fp.
- **H70 (the model reduces to milestone 14).** With an elastic-perfectly-plastic backbone yielding at 0.01 mm, the peak load matches `racking.capacity` within 0.5% for the intact sheet, the Weiss pattern and 200 random miss patterns. Refuted otherwise (a bug, not a finding).
- **H71 (spacing table).** At the measured backbone (monotonic, δp 12 mm), the intact sheet's capacity at 4, 3 and 2 in edge spacing relative to 6 in lies within 10% of SDPWS's ratios (1.536, 1.964, 2.607). Refuted otherwise.
- **H72 (softening explains Weiss).** Omitting every nail below 12 in loses at least 40% of the intact sheet's peak at every backbone in the sweep (δp 6–15 mm, monotonic and cyclic Δu). Refuted otherwise.
- **H73 (escaped misses unchanged).** Three reads on measured studs, every miss escaping, at the measured backbone: the mean sheet's loss and its 99th-percentile loss are each within 1 point of milestone 14's (2.34% and 11.5%). Refuted otherwise.
- **H74 (4 in joint edges still carry the misses).** With the measured backbone, the 4 in joint-edge schedule with every miss escaping keeps at least the intact 6 in sheet's peak in 99% of sheets. Refuted otherwise.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the drift at peak; the Weiss loss against δp and Δu; how far the sheet's centre of rotation moves; the fraction of nails past their peak at the sheet's peak; the absolute peak in kN/m at the tested Fp, beside SDPWS's tabulated value for a heavier panel (not a like-for-like check); the single-nail losses.
- **Limits known in advance.** Monotonic loading only (no cyclic degradation, which is how most shear-wall tests are run); pinned framing, rigid sheet, full anchorage, as in milestone 14; one nail-joint study, SPF framing (Weiss's framing species not read); the slip at peak is swept, not measured.

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 15 result: stud-to-plate joints at NDS strength do not explain Weiss's walls; they would need half that strength, or no hold-downs

`experiments/m15_frame_joints/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `src/buildbot_sheathing/frame_lp.py` (the framed sheet as a linear programme over rigid-body motions of the top plate, four studs and the sheet; the NDS yield-limit equations); `tests/test_frame_lp.py` (5 tests). 11 min on one CPU (about 4,000 LPs).

- **The NDS ratio.** An 8d common through 15/32 in OSB into G 0.50 framing yields at Z = 74.0 lb (mode IIIs); a 16d common through a 1.5 in plate at 140.7 lb (mode IV, which matches the 141 lb of NDS Table 12N); two end nails at the end-grain factor 0.67 give 188.5 lb, so ρ = 2.55 sheathing nails (2.34 in SPF).
- **H65 held.** With rigid joints the LP's capacity ratios match milestone 14's within 0.09% over 200 miss patterns and the Weiss pattern (0.7970 against 0.7963). Its absolute intact capacity is 1.2% above milestone 14's because the joint-edge nails sit 3/8 in off the stud centreline, so a rotating stud also slips them vertically; with the studs moved under the nails it agrees to 0.04%.
- **H66 refuted, for a reason the plan did not foresee.** At the NDS joints the intact 6 in sheet loses 7.2% (3.9–7.2% across 6–2 in spacing), and the spacing check fails (2 in: 2.87 against SDPWS's 2.61). The mechanism is not the stud joints sliding: a lone sheet's top plate tilts (0.18 rad per unit slide) while an interior stud's bottom lifts off its plate, which costs nothing when end-grain withdrawal is zero. Withdrawal of 2 F per joint, or (post hoc, section H) a top plate that only slides, as a continuous double top plate over a wall line would hold it, removes the loss (0.00% at 6 and 4 in, −1.5% at 2 in).
- **H67 refuted, robustly.** At the NDS joints the Weiss pattern loses 21.1% (19.7–21.1% for any withdrawal 0–2 F; 20.3% with the sliding top plate), essentially milestone 14's 20.4%: the joints are strong enough that the studs never slide. A 40% loss needs ρ ≤ 1.42 (0.56 of the NDS ratio) and 50% needs ρ ≤ 1.18 (0.46). Without hold-downs (not scored) the same sheet loses 64% at a withdrawal of 1 F and all of it at 0; the intact sheet without hold-downs keeps only 18–29%.
- **H68 refuted as registered; holds with a sliding top plate.** Three reads on measured studs, every miss escaping, at the registered joints: mean loss 2.73% (milestone 14: 2.34%), 99th-percentile 16.7% (11.5%), because the tilting-top-plate mechanism loads the joint edges harder (a joint-edge nail is worth 2.38% instead of 2.08%, a field nail 0.27% instead of 0.85%). With the top plate held to a slide: 2.34% and 11.51%, within 0.02 points of milestone 14.
- **H69 held.** 4 in joint edges keep at least the intact 6 in sheet's capacity in 99.89% of sheets (intact 1.365 relative to the registered intact 6 in sheet, minimum 0.710), and 99.98% with the sliding top plate.
- **What it means for the programme.** Milestone 14's rigid-plastic model is not unconservative because of the stud-to-plate joints at code strength; the gap to Weiss's walls is somewhere else: the anchorage (the model loses 64–100% without hold-downs), joints or fasteners far weaker at ultimate than the NDS ratio implies, or softening nails, none of which this model can choose between without Weiss's test set-up. So milestone 14's post-hoc 2.45 scaling stays a bracket, not a correction. Two things are firm: an escaped miss's cost depends on the top plate's restraint (a sheet whose top plate can tilt loses half as much again at the tail), and the 4 in joint-edge schedule carries the misses under every joint model tried.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the NDS ratio is a design-level (yield-limit) ratio, not tested ultimates; joints are rigid-plastic; friction at stud bearing and hold-down shear are ignored. Weiss's wall size, anchorage and framing were not read (abstract only; the thesis host does not resolve from this session).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 15 planned: do the framing joints explain why the model understated Weiss's walls?

Written before any milestone-15 capacity was computed. Inputs: milestone 14's nail layout, miss patterns (20,000 sheets, seed 7) and its intact-sheet reference.

- **Why.** Milestone 14's rigid-plastic model lost 20% of a sheet's capacity with every nail below 12 in omitted, where Weiss's (2017) tested walls lost "close to or more than half" (H61 refuted), so every escaped-miss loss it reported is a lower bound of unknown slack, and its post-hoc fix scaled every loss by 2.45. One reason the model cannot see: it pins the studs to the plates with joints of unlimited strength. With the bottom-plate sheathing nails gone, the racking load has to leave the sheet through the studs and reach the bottom plate through each stud's two end nails, which are weak in shear (end grain). If that is the reason, the loss is a property of *which* nails are missing (the bottom-plate row), not a uniform factor, and scattered joint-edge misses keep milestone 14's small losses.
- **Model.** A rigid-body limit analysis of the whole framed sheet, solved as a linear programme (upper bound: the least dissipation over kinematically admissible small motions with the top plate moved one unit along the wall). Bodies: the bottom plate (fixed), the top plate, each stud and the sheet, each with (u, v, θ). Sheathing nails: rigid-plastic, capacity F along their slip (the Euclidean norm, a 64-sided polygon). Stud-to-plate joints, top and bottom: two 16d common end nails (IRC Table R602.3(1)), lateral capacity ρF with ρ from the NDS yield-limit equations (dowel bearing strength 16,600 G^1.84 psi, nail bending yield by diameter, end-grain factor 0.67 on the end nail; the 8d sheathing nail through 15/32 in OSB at G 0.50 into G 0.50 framing), and swept 0.25–8 because the yield model is a design-level ratio, not a measured ultimate; they bear in compression and open in tension against a withdrawal capacity (end-grain withdrawal is not allowed in NDS: 0, swept to ρF). Hold-downs at the sheet's end studs fix them vertically (fully anchored); a no-hold-down case is reported, not scored.
- **H65 (the LP reproduces milestone 14).** With rigid (equality) stud-to-plate joints the LP's capacity ratios match `racking.capacity` within 0.5% for the intact sheet, the Weiss pattern and 200 random miss patterns from the three-read cell. Refuted otherwise (a bug, not a finding).
- **H66 (finite joints leave the intact sheet alone).** At the NDS ρ, the intact sheet's capacity at 6, 4, 3 and 2 in edge spacing changes by less than 2% each from milestone 14's, so H60 still holds. Refuted otherwise.
- **H67 (finite joints explain Weiss).** At the NDS ρ, omitting every nail below 12 in loses at least 40% of the intact sheet's capacity. Refuted otherwise.
- **H68 (scattered misses stay small).** At the NDS ρ, with every miss escaping (three reads, measured studs), the mean sheet's loss and its 99th-percentile loss are within 1 point of milestone 14's (2.34% and 11.5%), so milestone 14's post-hoc 2.45 scaling overstated them. Refuted otherwise.
- **H69 (the 4 in joint edges still carry the misses).** At the NDS ρ, milestone 14's 4 in joint-edge schedule with every miss escaping keeps at least the intact 6 in sheet's capacity in 99% of sheets. Refuted otherwise.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the ρ at which the Weiss loss reaches 40% and 50%; the loss of a single missed bottom-plate nail; the mechanism the LP picks (stud sliding on the plate, or racking); no hold-downs; doubled joint studs as two members.
- **Limits known in advance.** Weiss's walls' size, anchorage and framing were not read (only the abstract; the thesis host is unreachable from this session), so H67 compares against a qualitative "close to or more than half". The yield model gives design-level ratios, not tested ultimates; the joints are rigid-plastic (no stiffness, no softening); friction at the stud bearing and hold-down shear are ignored, which favours larger losses.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 14 result: an escaped miss costs about 2% of the sheet; misses cluster, and 4 in joint nailing covers them for less than a re-drive

`experiments/m14_escaped/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `src/buildbot_sheathing/racking.py`; `tack.simulate` gains `u_joint` and `return_hits`, passed through `ceiling.clean`; `tests/test_racking.py` (5 tests). 9 s to run. Milestone 12's cells reproduce (0.4253 clean and 1.095 missed for three reads; 0.7707 for the full stack). The plastic-work minimum is solved by reweighted least squares and matches Nelder–Mead to 1e-13.

- **H60 held.** Against SDPWS Table 4.3A (15/32 in Structural I, 8d; 280, 430, 550, 730 plf), the model's intact-sheet ratios at 4, 3 and 2 in edge spacing are 1.442, 1.884 and 2.769 against 1.536, 1.964 and 2.607 (−6%, −4%, +6%). The table values were read from secondary copies (a state code reprint, a manufacturer's tech tip quoting SDPWS 2021), not the standard itself.
- **H61 refuted, and it matters.** Omitting every nail below 12 in (11 of 62) removes 20.4% of the model's capacity (23.7% at ≤ 12 in), against Weiss's (2017) tested walls, which lost "close to or more than half". The rigid-plastic model is unconservative for clustered missing nails by a factor of about 2.5 there. Possible reasons, not tested: the tests' walls may not have been fully anchored, a sheet with no bottom-edge nails rocks and tears out at the next row, and real nails are not rigid-plastic. Every loss below is therefore a lower bound.
- **H62 held.** One joint-edge nail missed alone costs 2.00–2.21% of the sheet (mean 2.08%; corners most), a field nail 0.70–1.02%, a plate nail 1.05–1.83%. The 62 single losses sum to 1.024, so a nail carries about its share by count, weighted toward the perimeter.
- **H63 refuted.** Three reads on measured studs, every miss escaping: the mean sheet loses 2.34% (2.13% per miss), but the 99th-percentile sheet loses 11.5% (bar 10%), and a 16 ft wall line's 1st percentile keeps 93.4% (bar 95%). The misses cluster: 13.4% of sheets have three or more joint misses (worst: 11), because a stud the robot misjudges is misjudged for a run of heights. The full stack (0.771 clean) loses 0.63% on average, 6.3% at the 99th percentile; doubled joints lose nothing.
- **H64 held.** Nailing the two joint edges at 4 in (25 a side; plates 6 in, field 12 in) raises the intact sheet to 1.318 of the 6 in sheet; with 1.572 misses a sheet all escaping, 99.98% of sheets still carry at least the intact 6 in sheet's capacity (1st percentile 1.160, worst 0.868). Post hoc, with every loss scaled by Weiss's 2.45, 97.6% do and the 1st percentile is 0.930.
- **Also found.** Sixteen extra nails at milestone 7's robot seconds per nail cost less than re-driving 1.1 misses a sheet: at 100 houses a year the 4 in joint edges with misses left cost a median $6.98 per sheet against $7.10 for 6 in with every miss re-driven and $12.17 for doubled joints (cheaper than doubled joints in all 4,000 draws). Nail material is not priced (a few cents). Miss rates by height run 1.8–4.4% a nail, highest at the ends and at 42–54 in, which is where the measured studs' waviness sits.
- **What it means for the programme.** Milestone 13 asked what a missed nail costs; in capacity, about 2% of its sheet each, more when they cluster, and the model likely understates clustered losses. For a shear wall, a design that expects misses can carry them: 4 in nailing on single-2x joint edges keeps the 6 in schedule's capacity with every miss left in, for no more than re-driving. Whether a code official accepts a schedule that knowingly includes shiners is a separate question this cannot answer; the honest framing is that it is a robot-friendly design margin, not a licence to skip inspection.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the rigid-plastic fastener (equal capacity, no edge-distance or tear-out effects), full anchorage, sheets acting independently; the seconds of an added nail (t_nail / 62).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 14 planned: what an escaped missed nail does to the sheet's racking capacity

Written before any milestone-14 capacity was computed. Inputs: milestone 12's pipeline (`ceiling.clean`, measured studs, 110 mm mean, seed 7), now asked for which nails miss, not how many.

- **Why.** Milestone 13 turned the framing question into a question about missed nails: doubled joints pay only if a missed nail costs about $5.51 more than its re-drive. Every milestone so far assumed each miss is found and re-driven. A miss that escapes (a "shiner" beside the stud) is a fastener missing from a shear wall's schedule, and its cost depends on how much racking capacity it takes away. That is computable without a price, as a fraction of the sheet's capacity, from where the misses fall: single-2x misses sit on the two joint edges, and a fastener's share of a sheet's capacity depends on its place.
- **Model.** The rigid-plastic model of a fully anchored sheathed wall (Källsner and Girhammar 2009, as we read it): framing pinned at its joints, so a stud nail at height z moves γz along the wall and a top-plate nail γH; the sheet rigid (translation u, v and rotation θ); every fastener rigid-plastic with the same capacity F in the direction of its slip. The racking capacity is the least work over the sheet's rigid motions, H·γ·H_wall = F·min Σ|slip_i|, so a capacity *ratio* needs only the nail positions, not F. Nails: SDPWS's 6 in edge and 12 in field schedule as the programme already places them (17 per joint edge, 7 per field stud, 7 per plate, 62 in all). A wall line is the sum of its sheets (they share γ). Misses come from `tack.simulate`, now returning the hit pattern of each sheet.
- **H60 (the model reproduces the spacing table).** The intact sheet's capacity at 4, 3 and 2 in edge spacing (field 12 in), relative to 6 in, lies within 10% of SDPWS Table 4.3A's ratios for 15/32 in Structural I with 8d common nails (430, 550 and 730 plf against 280: 1.536, 1.964, 2.607). Refuted if any is outside.
- **H61 (the model agrees with a test of missing nails).** Weiss (2017, MS thesis, WSU) omitted every sheathing nail in the bottom 12 in of tested walls to simulate decay, and the walls lost "close to or more than half" their capacity. The model, omitting every nail below 12 in, loses at least 40%. Refuted otherwise (the model would then be unconservative for clustered misses).
- **H62 (a missed nail costs about its share).** For each of the 34 joint-edge nails missed alone, the sheet loses at most 4% of its capacity, and the mean over the 34 lies between 1% and 3% (1/62 = 1.6%). Refuted otherwise.
- **H63 (escaped misses at the simulated rate are small).** Single 2x on measured studs, three reads, ledger (milestone 12: 0.425 clean, 1.095 missed a sheet), every miss escaping: the mean sheet loses under 3% of capacity and the 99th-percentile sheet under 10%, and a four-sheet (16 ft) wall line keeps at least 95% of its intact capacity in 99% of walls. Refuted if any of the three fails.
- **H64 (misses can be designed out with nails).** The same pipeline with its two joint edges nailed at 4 in (25 nails each; plates 6 in, field 12 in) and every miss escaping keeps at least the intact 6 in sheet's capacity in 99% of sheets. Refuted otherwise.
- **Also reported, not scored:** the capacity lost by misses on the best single-2x stack (per-nail reads, push, slide and shim; 0.771 clean) and on doubled joints; which joint heights miss most and what each height's nail is worth; the extra nailing seconds and dollars of H64's denser joint edges in milestone 7's cost model, against doubled joints' $5.13 premium; the break-even of milestone 13 restated as a capacity.
- **Limits known in advance.** A rigid-plastic model with equal fasteners: no stiffness, no sheathing tear-out, no nail-to-edge-distance effects, no partial anchorage; a shiner is assumed to carry nothing; sheet-to-sheet interaction at a shared joint stud is ignored (each sheet has its own edge nails); one wall of six measured studs drives the misses. The model ranks miss patterns and sizes their effect relative to an intact sheet; it does not give a design value.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 13 result: the remedies cost 32 cents a clean sheet; the doubled joint stud costs $5.13 and still loses every draw

`experiments/m13_price/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `src/buildbot_sheathing/price.py`; `tests/test_price.py` (5 tests). 5 s to run. One new Monte Carlo cell (doubled joints, single read, measured studs: 1.0000 clean, 0 missed); everything else reuses milestone 12's clean sheets and milestone 7's 4,000 cost draws.

- **H57 held.** At 100 houses a year the per-draw cheapest single-2x pipeline beats doubled joints in 1.000 of draws (bar 0.90), and also at 300 and 1,000; the median saving is $5.13 a sheet. Doubled joints are the fastest pipeline (145 s against 198–304 s) but the stud and a framer's time are 44% of their $12.17.
- **H58 held.** The full stack (per-nail reads by line laser, mid push, slide and shim; 0.771 clean) costs a median $7.42 per clean sheet against $7.10 for three reads on the ledger (0.425 clean), and is the cheapest pipeline in none of the 4,000 draws. Interpretation note: the plan said "the cheapest single-2x pipeline"; since a per-draw minimum can never be beaten by one of its members, it was scored against the lowest-median pipeline. Three reads on the ledger is cheapest in 52% of draws, per-nail reads by line laser on the ledger in 26%, three reads with slide and shim in 14%.
- **H59 refuted.** The extra cost a missed nail must carry for doubled joints to tie is a median $5.51 [5–95%: $3.91, $8.55] (8.8 loaded person-minutes), down from milestone 7's $6.51 but above the $5.00 bar; $5.83 with the common part removed. It fell less than the missed nails rose (0.79 to 1.095) because in about half the draws the cheapest single-2x pipeline is one that misses fewer nails (per-nail reads, 0.765; slide and shim, 0.726), which shrinks the denominator: the median saving over 1.095 alone would give $4.68.
- **Also found.** Below the base's capacity, seconds cost only supervision, so the price of first-pass quality is flat up to 0.77 (the cheapest pipeline at 0.40, 0.50, 0.60 and 0.75 first-pass clean costs $7.10, $7.14, $7.24 and $7.42) and then jumps to $12.17 for 1.000. A line laser beats three point reads at per-nail resolution above about 0.23 m/s; per-nail reads by point cost 15 cents and 31 s more than three reads.
- **What it means for the programme.** In dollars, the paper's question about framing is now a question about missed nails: if a missed nail found at inspection (or not found) costs more than about $5.50 beyond its re-drive, double the joint; otherwise run single 2x with whichever remedies the line allows, since they are nearly free. The next input to get is that cost and the detection rate of a missed nail, not more sensing.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the slide-and-shim time, the sweep speed and the push time (all swept); every missed nail assumed found.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 13 planned: what the single-2x remedies cost, and whether doubling pays on measured studs

Written before any milestone-13 pipeline was priced. Inputs are milestone 12's committed `results.json` (clean sheets and missed nails for the 12 single-2x cells on measured studs and the 12 with the common part removed) and milestone 7's cost model and 4,000 draws (seed 11).

- **Why.** Milestone 7 found doubled joints never pay in dollars unless a missed nail costs about $6.51 more than its re-drive, but it priced parabolic low-warp studs (0.79 missed nails a sheet with three reads). On measured studs the same pipeline misses 1.09 nails a sheet, and the remedies of milestones 9, 10 and 12 (per-nail reads, a mid push, a slide and shim) each buy clean sheets with seconds milestone 7 never charged. Milestone 12 left the per-nail read's time open. The question a panel-line integrator asks: which single-2x pipeline is cheapest per clean sheet on real studs, and is the extra joint stud now worth it?
- **Model.** Milestone 7's G1 on a drive-and-lift base (the cheapest robot class), 100, 300 and 1,000 houses a year; every missed nail found and re-driven (t_fix). Seconds added per sheet: point reads at milestone 7's t_read U(1, 3) s each (three: 3 per stud; dense: 17 per stud; local: 17 on the joint stud and 7 on each of two field studs; plus 2 edge reads); or, for dense and local, a line-laser sweep along the three new studs, 3 × 2.353 m / v with v log-uniform 0.05–0.5 m/s (unsourced; ISO 10218-1's 250 mm/s reduced speed sits inside it) plus the 2 edge reads; the mid push U(10, 40) s (milestone 9); slide and shim U(5, 30) s plus 2 more edge reads (unsourced, swept). Doubled joints: single read, ledger, the extra stud and a framer's time (milestone 7), clean sheets and misses from a new 20,000-sheet run on the measured studs.
- **H57 (doubling still does not pay).** At 100 houses a year, the cheapest single-2x pipeline on measured studs (chosen per draw over all 12 cells and both read hardwares) is cheaper than doubled joints in at least 90% of draws. Refuted otherwise.
- **H58 (the remedies buy first-pass quality, not dollars).** In the median draw at 100 houses a year, the full stack (local reads by line laser, mid push, slide and shim; 0.771 clean) costs more per clean sheet than the cheapest single-2x pipeline. Held if it costs more; refuted otherwise.
- **H59 (measured studs lower the break-even).** The median extra cost a missed nail must carry, beyond its re-drive, for doubled joints to tie the per-draw cheapest single-2x pipeline is below $5.00 (milestone 7: $6.51 on parabolic studs). Refuted otherwise.
- **Also reported, not scored:** median dollars and seconds of every pipeline; the cost of first-pass quality (the cheapest pipeline reaching each clean-sheet level); the line-laser speed at which per-nail reads stop paying for themselves; the common-removed studs as a bracket; 300 and 1,000 houses a year, where the machine's capacity can bind.
- **Limits known in advance.** The slide-and-shim time and the sweep speed are unsourced and swept; missed nails are all assumed found, so an escaped miss (a shear-wall nailing defect) has no price here, which is exactly what H59's break-even asks the reader to supply; one robot-built wall's six studs.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 12 result: per-nail reads and the mid push both survive measured studs; together with slide and shim they reach 0.77, not 0.90

`experiments/m12_remedies/` (run.py, results.txt, results.json, figure.py, figure.png); `tack.simulate` gains a `local` read policy and a mid push on any stud shape; `measured.model_profiles` holds milestone 11's profile variants; `ceiling.clean` takes `profiles`; `tests/test_remedies.py` (5 tests). 39 s to run. Milestone 11's three cells reproduce to four decimals (0.4253, 0.4707, 0.5792).

- **A bug in the first run, fixed before any result was used.** Milestone 11's profiles are measured about each stud's least-squares line, not the chord through its ends; the first run took the push from the least-squares line, so the push moved a stud by a third of its bow in the wrong place and made things worse (three reads 0.425 to 0.428; the end-chord RMS of five studs went up). The registered model says "offset from its chord" and the push is physically pinned at the plates, so the push now uses the end chord (`test_push_is_measured_from_the_end_chord`). The results below are from the fixed code only; the parabola and straight cells were unaffected (their profiles are zero at the ends).
- **H53 held: most of the measured-stud loss is the estimate.** Reading the stud at every nail height and aiming at each read gives 0.530 on measured studs (ledger, no push), above the 0.502 bar; the waviness cost falls from 15.4 points (three reads) and 12.5 (dense parabola) to 4.7. On the parabola and straight studs per-nail reads gain nothing (0.577 and 0.813 against 0.579 and 0.811): their only use is waviness.
- **H54 held: the push still pays.** A mid push (three reads, ledger) lifts measured studs from 0.425 to 0.552 (+12.6 points; bar +10), against +16.3 on their own parabolas (0.579 to 0.742). Pushed onto the end chord, four of the six studs straighten (end-chord RMS 0.83–3.49 mm to 0.45–1.20) and the most leaning end stud gets worse (0.94 to 1.56), because its shape is not a bow. Forces 10–72 N at a mean Stud-grade E, inside milestone 9's range.
- **H55 refuted: the remedies stack, but to 0.77.** Per-nail reads, the mid push and a 0.5 mm slide plus shim give 0.771 on measured studs (0.796 with the common part removed), against 0.860 on their own parabolas and 0.92 on straight studs. 95% then needs a tool held to 0.61 mm (1σ; 0.63 with the common part removed) against 0.84 mm on straight studs; three reads with the push need 0.27 mm.
- **H56 held.** Doubled joints give 1.0000 in all six estimate-placement cells on measured studs.
- **What it means for the programme.** The single-2x route is now: read at every nail (a line laser along the stud, not three spots), push the joint stud to its end chord, slide and shim the sheet, and hold the tool to about 0.6 mm. Each step is needed and none is enough alone. Doubled joints still need none of it. The paper's single-2x recommendation narrows accordingly.
- **Inputs not sourced:** the slide and shim residuals (0.5 mm, milestone 10's sweep), the push error (= the 0.5 mm read), linear superposition of the push on the scanned shape, and the length-squared scaling from 2,220 mm.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 12 planned: do the single-2x remedies survive measured studs?

Written before any milestone-12 pipeline was run. Inputs are milestone 11's committed `profiles.json` and its 110 mm running mean.

- **Why.** Milestone 11 found the six measured studs are not parabolas, and the best single-2x pipeline drops from 0.579 (each stud's own parabola) to 0.425 on their measured shapes. Milestones 9 and 10 proposed two remedies, a mid-height push onto the line and a slide plus shim before tacking (0.92–0.94 on straight studs), both scored only on parabolic or straight studs. Milestone 11 also left open whether the loss is the robot's *estimate* (a parabola fitted to three or seventeen reads cannot follow the waviness) or the *geometry* (a straight sheet edge against a wavy face narrows the window however well it is known). These are different fixes: more sensing, or more framing.
- **Model.** Milestone 11's pipeline (single 2x, smooth-rock Drake seats, tool 0.9 mm, reads 0.5 mm, edge aim, 20,000 sheets, seed 7) on four stud sets: measured (110 mm mean), measured with the cross-member common part removed, each stud's own parabola, and straight. Three switches: (1) the estimate, *three* (parabola through three reads), *dense* (parabola through 17) or a new *local* policy that reads the stud at every nail height (17 on a joint stud, 7 on a field stud) and aims each nail at its own read, no fitted shape; (2) a mid-height push, generalised to any measured shape by superposition: the stud moves by (its mid-height offset from its chord + N(0, s_read)) times the pinned point-load shape, and the robot believes its own estimate moved by its estimated offset times the same shape; (3) placement, milestone 9's ledger seat or milestone 10's slide plus shim (0.5 mm residuals). Doubled joints on the measured studs as a check.
- **H53 (the loss is mostly the estimate).** On measured studs (110 mm mean), ledger placement, no push, *local* reads close at least half of the gap from three-read measured (0.425) to three-read own-parabola (0.579), i.e. reach 0.502 or more. Refuted otherwise.
- **H54 (the push still pays).** On measured studs, three reads, ledger placement, the mid push lifts clean sheets by at least 10 points over no push (milestone 9 found +15 on parabolic low-warp studs). Refuted otherwise.
